```text
[DJ Mauch] Make this podcast:

# THE OVERVIEW

### Episode: Your Next AI Conversation Might Happen In Space

*A short podcast about why some of the biggest companies on Earth are trying to leave it. Approx. 12 minutes.*

-----

[INTRO MUSIC FADES IN, THEN UNDER]

MAYA: Hey, welcome back to *The Overview*. I’m Maya.

SAM: And I’m Sam. Maya, I want to start today with a question. What would you say if I told you that right now, this minute, some of the biggest companies in the world — Google, SpaceX, Amazon, Nvidia — are in a quiet race to build data centers in space?

MAYA: I’d say… you got that off a movie poster.

SAM: *[laughs]* Right? It sounds made up. But it’s real. There are already small computers running in orbit, doing the kind of work that data centers do down here. And the long-term plan is wildly bigger than that. SpaceX has filed paperwork — actual paperwork with regulators — to launch up to a million satellites just for this.

MAYA: A *million*.

SAM: A million.

MAYA: Okay.

SAM: So today’s episode is just: what is this thing? Why is it happening? And what does it have to do with the rest of us walking around down here? You don’t need a tech background. We’re going to keep it normal.

MAYA: Good. Because when you say “data center in space,” I’m picturing the International Space Station with a bunch of laptops duct-taped to the wall.

[BEAT]

SAM: Okay let’s start there. A data center on Earth — when you ask ChatGPT something, or watch Netflix, or send a Gmail — all of that runs through these giant buildings full of computers. They look like warehouses from the outside. Inside, picture a library, except every book is a computer thinking really fast and getting really, really hot.

MAYA: How hot?

SAM: Hot enough that the biggest cost after the electricity to *run* the computers is the electricity to *cool* them. And they drink water like you wouldn’t believe. The newest AI data centers use millions of gallons a day, just to keep the chips from melting.

MAYA: Per day?

SAM: Per day. And that’s starting to be a real problem. Some towns are pushing back. Some power grids — Northern Virginia, Singapore, parts of Europe — they’re tapped out. So the question a few of these companies started asking is: what if we just… didn’t put them on Earth?

MAYA: What if we just sent the warehouse to space.

SAM: Basically, yeah. And when you sit with it, space has some weirdly perfect features for this.

[BEAT]

MAYA: Alright, sell me. Why is space good for computers?

SAM: Three reasons. The first one — the sun never sets up there. If you put your satellite in the right orbit, you can have your solar panels pointed at the sun twenty-four hours a day. No clouds. No night. No weather.

MAYA: Wait. So a solar panel in space is doing the work of —

SAM: Way more than one on a roof. The sunlight is also more intense, because there’s no atmosphere in the way filtering it out. A solar panel in the right orbit produces something like eight to ten times what the same panel does in the best spot on Earth.

MAYA: Okay. Reason two.

SAM: Cooling. Fun fact: space is cold. Deep, dark, almost-absolute-zero cold.

MAYA: Which is the coldest a thing can physically be.

SAM: Right. So in theory you’ve got the world’s biggest freezer sitting right outside your window. You don’t have to pay for it. You don’t have to pipe water through it. It’s just there.

MAYA: That sounds too good. What’s the catch?

SAM: There’s a catch. We’ll come back to it. But the basic idea: if you can get rid of the heat, you can pack way more computing into way less space.

MAYA: And reason three?

SAM: No neighbors. No zoning meetings. No town hall where people are mad you’re building a warehouse next to the elementary school. No farmland getting paved over. No water table getting drained. Up there, nobody cares.

MAYA: Just stars and the occasional dead satellite.

SAM: Exactly.

[BEAT]

MAYA: Okay. Free sunlight, free cooling, nobody to fight with. Why is anyone still building data centers on Earth?

SAM: *[laughs]* Great question. So — the catches. A few of them.
The first is just that getting stuff to space is expensive. It’s getting cheaper because of reusable rockets, but you’re still paying serious money for every pound you lift up there.

MAYA: And computers are heavy.

SAM: Computers are heavy. And the high-end AI chips — the ones everyone wants right now — they were designed to sit in a clean, air-conditioned room, not to take a rocket ride and then survive radiation for ten years. So you have to either toughen them up for space, or build new ones from scratch. Nvidia actually just announced a chip line designed specifically for orbit, earlier this year.

MAYA: Okay. What else?

SAM: Cooling is harder than I made it sound. Yes, space is cold — but space is also a vacuum. And in a vacuum there’s no *air* to carry heat away. On Earth, you blow cold air past a hot chip, the air picks up the heat, you cool the air, you do it again. In space, no air. So instead you have to dump the heat by *radiating* it — basically by glowing infrared into the dark.

MAYA: So you can use space as a freezer, but only if you’ve got a really big radiator.

SAM: Right. And the size of your radiator caps how powerful your computer can be. Engineers are obsessed with this problem.

MAYA: What else?

SAM: When something breaks, you can’t just walk over and fix it. There’s no IT guy in orbit. So the hardware has to either last forever, or be cheap enough you write it off when it dies.

And there’s one more — depending on what the computer is doing, you might need to get the answer back to Earth fast. If a satellite snaps a picture and an AI on board figures out “oh, that’s a forest fire” — great. Beam it down. But if you’re training a giant AI model and the training data is all on Earth, the round trip is slow.

MAYA: So it’s not great for everything.

SAM: Not for everything, not yet. And honestly — there are skeptics. There’s a startup called Varda

## What I captured

You gave feedback on **65 episodes**:

Saved human-readable calibration rules here:

## Main lesson

The Qwen filter was directionally useful but **too generous**, especially with `listen`.

Your actual preference is more like:

- **Listen:** scarce, original-audio-worthy
- **Summarize:** broad daily digest candidate
- **Scan:** maybe interesting, learn more first
- **Skip:** omit from digest unless calibration/debug

## Calibration examples now encoded

### Promote / listen-type signals

- Mercor CEO on AI labor economics / model-vs-app-layer defensibility
- Dara / Uber AV + marketplace strategy
- Gita Gopinath on global rates / AI capex inflation
- Ranjan Roy on AI boom warning signs
- Toast business breakdown

### Summarize, but don’t necessarily listen

- Fivetran / enterprise data infra for agents
- Axiom Math
- Operator-led PE
- RenMac market models
- Mark Pincus product frameworks
- Evolutionary AI models
- Personal AI workflows / second brain
- Terry Sejnowski
- China vs Nvidia

### Penalize / skip despite AI relevance

- Exa search infra promo
- Continual-learning technical research
- Video/generative-media agent demos
- Generic AI consciousness/alarmism
- Vertical vendor AI stories like Aircall/Mitel/elder care
- Corgi culture promo

- Consumer brand playbooks

### assistant

```text
Yes — you’re thinking about this exactly right.

First: this is **Railway disk/volume storage**, not RAM memory. It won’t “kill memory” directly, but if we keep raw JSON/Markdown/transcripts every day forever, it will bloat the persistent `/opt/data` volume and make backups/syncs annoying.

Current size is tiny:

- `/opt/data/podcast_digest`: **1.2 MB**
- Today’s largest files:
  - full Qwen 100-episode JSON: ~206 KB each
  - full calibration markdown: ~86 KB each
  - daily 24h digest txt/md: ~10 KB

So we’re fine now, but we should design retention before automation.

## Recommended storage model

### Keep permanently

These are durable and useful:

1. **Taste calibration**
   - What you liked/disliked.
   - Scoring rules.
   - Guest/show/topic preferences.
   - Examples of false positives / false negatives.

2. **Idea index**
   - Durable concepts you liked.
   - Example: “AI token budgeting as new corporate cost discipline.”
   - Example: “Private evals as enterprise moat.”
   - Example: “Idiot Index as business simplification framework.”

3. **Small episode metadata database with retention**
   - Enough to avoid duplicates.
   - Enough to know what was already sent.
   - Not full transcripts forever.

### Keep temporarily

These should auto-expire:

1. **Raw daily Qwen scores**
   - Keep 7–14 days max.
   - Useful for debugging only.

2. **Raw daily markdown calibration files**
   - Keep 7–14 days max.

3. **Full transcripts**
   - Keep 3–7 days max, or don’t persist at all.
   - If a transcript creates a durable idea, save the idea, not the transcript.

4. **Rendered daily digest files**
   - Keep maybe 30 days max, or just deliver to Telegram and store a compact digest record.

## Proposed directory structure

``​`text
/opt/data/podcast_digest/
  config/
    feeds.yaml
    resolved_feeds.yaml
    guest_watchlist.yaml
    skip_rules.yaml

  db/
    episodes.sqlite
    ideas.sqlite
    calibration.sqlite

  outputs/
    latest-daily-digest.txt
    latest-weekly-audio.mp3

  archive/
    daily/
      keep only 14-30 days

  tmp/
    qwen_scores/
    transcripts/
    scratch/
    auto-delete after 7 days
``​`

Right now we have everything in `outputs/`, which is fine for prototype but not for live.

## What I think we should persist

### 1. `ideas.sqlite`

A durable “idea index” with rows like:

``​`text
idea_id
date_seen
source_episode
show
guest
idea_title
idea_summary
why_dj_cares
category
strength
status
``​`

Examples:

- “Token spend will exceed headcount spend”
- “Private evals as enterprise AI moat”
- “Agent access to systems of record becomes new data-control fight”
- “AI capex creates global rate/inflation pressure”
- “Idiot Index as cost decomposition tool”

This is the useful long-term memory.

### 2. `calibration.sqlite`

A durable preference-learning table:

``​`text
episode_id
title
show
qwen_score
qwen_tier
dj_feedback
final_label
reason_learned
created_at
``​`

This lets the model learn from your feedback without keeping every daily artifact.

### 3. `episodes.sqlite` with TTL

Keep episode metadata for maybe **60–90 days**, not forever.

Fields:

``​`text
episode_id
show
title
guest
published
link
summary
score
final_label
sent_to_dj
created_at
expires_at
``​`

We need this mostly for:
- deduping
- not sending same episode twice
- weekly rollups
- short-term audit/debug

## What we should not persist long-term

- full Qwen raw response files
- full daily scoring JSON
- full transcripts
- giant markdown calibration dumps
- audio scratch files
- intermediate prompt payloads

Those should either be deleted automatically or compressed/archived briefly.

## Suggested retention policy

For live automation:

- **Daily digest `.txt`:** keep 30 days
- **Raw scores JSON:** keep 7 days
- **Calibration/debug markdown:** keep 7 days
- **Transcripts:** keep 3 days, or delete after summary
- **Episode metadata:** keep 90 days
- **Idea index:** keep forever
- **User calibration/preferences:** keep forever
- **Weekly audio files:** keep maybe 8 weeks unless you want archive

## Practical next step

I should implement a cleanup/retention script before automation:

``​`bash
/opt/data/scripts/podcast_digest_cleanup.py
``​`

It would:

1. Delete raw Qwen score JSON older than 7 days.
2. Delete calibration markdown older than 7 days.
3. Delete transcript scratch files older than 3 days.
4. Keep only:
   - `latest-daily-digest.txt`
   - recent digest archive
   - durable SQLite databases
5. Vacuum SQLite occasionally.

Then for the actual daily flow, instead of saving a big file every day, we do:

- send digest directly to Telegram
- save compact digest to SQLite
- update idea index
- save `latest-daily-digest.txt`
- optionally keep a 14–30 day rolling archive

## Best design principle

**Persist learnings, not artifacts.**

Artifacts are daily exhaust. Learnings are:
- what DJ likes
- what DJ skips
- what ideas matter
- which guests/topics/shows are predictive
- which model mistakes repeat

That’s what should become durable.
```

{"success": true, "name": "podcast-intelligence-digest", "description": "Build, run, calibrate, and automate DJ's podcast conversation radar: global podcast discovery, episode ranking, daily text digests, and weekly audio briefings.", "tags": ["podcasts", "digest", "briefing", "discovery", "rss", "ranking", "audio-briefing"], "related_skills": ["podcast-audio-production", "youtube-content", "daily-business-briefing"], "content": "---\nname: podcast-intelligence-digest\ndescription: \"Build, run, calibrate, and automate DJ's podcast conversation radar: global podcast discovery, episode ranking, daily text digests, and weekly audio briefings.\"\nversion: 1.0.0\nauthor: Hermes Agent\nlicense: private\nmetadata:\n  hermes:\n    tags: [podcasts, digest, briefing, discovery, rss, ranking, audio-briefing]\n    related_skills: [podcast-audio-production, youtube-content, daily-business-briefing]\n---\n\n# Podcast Intelligence Digest\n\n## Overview\n\nUse this skill for DJ's \"global podcast conversation radar\": identifying the highest-signal podcast conversations from a broad source universe, ranking them against DJ's intellectual/professional taste, producing a concise daily text digest, and optionally turning the week's best conversations into a two-host audio digest.\n\nThe product is **not** a generic podcast newsletter. It should behave like a sharp analyst scanning the podcast world for conversations DJ would actually act on, listen to, or use to update a mental model.\n\n**Critical calibration rule:** score and evaluate **episodes**, not podcasts. Shows are source priors/discovery funnels only. A low-priority show can produce a must-listen episode; a high-priority show can produce a skip. Final recommendations must be episode-level.\n\n**Daily vs weekly distinction:** the daily text digest should cast a wider net and summarize what was said across more potentially relevant episodes. The weekly \"podcast of podcasts\" audio should be much more finely tuned: only the highest-signal, DJ-specific themes/conversations should make it into the script.\n\n**Storage principle:** persist learnings, not artifacts. Keep durable taste calibration, final labels, and an idea index; do not keep raw daily Qwen JSON, calibration markdown, transcripts, or scratch files forever. Daily outputs should be delivered as iPhone-friendly plain text and optionally kept only in a short rolling archive.\n\n**Calibration from DJ's first Qwen episode pass:** Qwen's first pass was too generous with `listen`. Treat `listen` as scarce original-audio-worthy, `digest` as worth summarizing, `scan` as investigate/brief mention, `skip` as omit. DJ explicitly liked/listened to: Mercor CEO on AI labor/model economics, Dara/Uber AV strategy, Gita Gopinath global rates, Ranjan Roy on AI boom warnings, Toast business breakdown, and Stratechery best-of content. DJ wanted summaries for enterprise data infrastructure/Fivetran agents, Axiom Math, operator-led PE, RenMac market models, Mark Pincus product frameworks, evolutionary AI models, personal AI workflows, Terry Sejnowski, and China vs Nvidia. DJ skipped high-scoring but too-narrow/noisy items like Exa search infra promo, continual-learning research, video/generative-media agent demos, generic AI consciousness/alarmism, vertical AI vendor stories, Corgi culture promo, Elon/media narrative, consumer brand playbooks, crypto chatter, and generic Bloomberg/news recaps.\n\n## When to Use\n\nUse when the user asks to:\n\n- build, run, debug, or calibrate a podcast digest/radar/monitor;\n- collect podcast episodes from RSS feeds;\n- rank episodes by guest/topic relevance;\n- produce a daily podcast intelligence brief;\n- script or generate a weekly audio digest from podcast highlights;\n- expand discovery beyond subscribed shows by searching for high-signal guests.\n\nIf the task is purely converting an already-written script into audio, use `podcast-audio-production` instead. If podcast audio generation is part of a weekly digest pipeline, use this skill first for selection/scripting, then `podcast-audio-production` for production.\n\n## DJ Relevance Test\n\nEvery candidate episode must pass this question:\n\n> Will this conversation give DJ an action, framework, market read, operator insight, allocation implication, or mental-model shift?\n\nPrioritize:\n\n- market/business shapers;\n- top investors, allocators, and CEOs;\n- AI leaders and frontier thinkers;\n- rare-access conversations with decision-makers;\n- operator details from scaled companies;\n- durable frameworks, not news chatter;\n- business model, capital allocation, software economics, AI platform, labor/productivity, and company-building implications.\n\nPenalize or skip:\n\n- news recaps and politics-first commentary;\n- celebrity/entertainment interviews;\n- generic VC/promotional fluff;\n- second-tier partner promo episodes;\n- shallow founder origin stories without reusable ideas;\n- generic AI hype with no operator/investor specificity;\n- energy/climate/defense/geopolitics unless DJ explicitly redirects the product.\n\n## Source Model\n\nMaintain a three-ring source universe:\n\n1. **Ring 1 — calibration subscriptions**: shows DJ already values. Use these to understand taste and baseline signal.\n2. **Ring 2 — high-signal shows**: shows likely to host relevant CEOs, investors, AI leaders, and framework thinkers.\n3. **Ring 3 — global guest discovery**: do not rely only on known shows. Search podcast indexes/web for watchlist people appearing anywhere.\n\nKeep a guest/watchlist file as the strongest identity signal, but do not let a guest name in unrelated footer/link text dominate ranking.\n\n## Default Workflow\n\n1. **Load current configuration and prior outputs.**\n   - Inspect source lists, watchlists, skip rules, previous digest outputs, and the episode database when present.\n   - Do not assume a cron job exists or is approved; check first.\n\n2. **Resolve RSS feeds.**\n   - Use Apple/iTunes search and manual

### assistant

```text
Yes — that critique is exactly right.

What I delivered was still too much like a **calibration artifact**: it showed the machinery, model behavior, skipped noise, and “Qwen got this wrong” notes. That’s useful while building, but it is *not* the product experience.

The actual briefing should feel like:

> “I scanned the relevant podcast universe, filtered it through your taste model, read deeper where warranted, and here are the conversations/ideas worth your attention.”

I updated the podcast briefing skill with this new production format.

## Proposed daily briefing structure

``​`text
Podcast Intelligence Briefing — Last 24 Hours

Top line
- 2–4 bullets synthesizing the most important ideas/themes from today’s podcast universe.

Must listen / weekly candidates
- Highest-scoring episodes likely to feed the weekly podcast-of-podcasts.
- Show, episode, guest, one-line thesis, why DJ should care, link.

Transcript summaries
- Episodes important enough to read via transcript.
- Main idea, key supporting points, action/framework/mental-model takeaway.

Lightweight summaries
- Interesting episodes where metadata/show notes were enough.
- Concise summary and why it made the cut.

Worth noting / monitor
- Weak but nonzero signal.
- New theme, new guest, or maybe-useful episode.

Bottom line counts
- Channels viewed: N
- Podcast episodes released: N
- Filtered out: N
- Flagged for lightweight summary: N
- Read via transcript: N
- Must listen / weekly candidates: N
``​`

## Key changes from the last version

### 1. Anything interesting makes it in

The output should not only show “top picks.” If something clears your relevance bar, it appears somewhere in the briefing.

Tiers become:

- **Must listen / weekly candidate**
- **Transcript summary**
- **Lightweight summary**
- **Worth noting / monitor**
- **Filtered out** — counted only, not listed unless you ask

### 2. Transcript reading is a middle layer

Not every interesting episode needs a transcript.

Pipeline should be:

1. **All released episodes** from relevant channels
2. **Filtered candidates** via metadata/show notes
3. **Interesting episodes** make briefing
4. **High-scoring subset** gets transcript read
5. **Best subset** becomes weekly podcast-of-podcasts candidates

### 3. Counts go at the bottom

The numbers are important, but they should be a confidence footer, not the main content.

Example:

``​`text
Coverage
Channels viewed: 54
Podcast episodes released: 21
Filtered out: 10
Flagged for lightweight summary: 8
Read via transcript: 3
Must listen / weekly candidates: 2
``​`

### 4. No calibration notes in production briefing

Unless you ask, the daily briefing should *not* say:

- “Qwen over-ranked this”
- “the ranker still fails here”
- “calibration note”
- “model cost”
- “debug funnel”

Those belong in internal logs or a separate calibration report.

## My proposed product logic

Daily briefing should be generated as:

``​`text
Universe scan
→ metadata scoring
→ DJ taste filter
→ interesting set
→ transcript expansion for top subset
→ final daily briefing
→ durable idea index update
→ weekly candidate queue update
``​`

## Daily output should answer

For each surfaced item:

1. **What is the conversation?**
2. **What is the core idea?**
3. **Why does DJ care?**
4. **What should DJ do with it?**
   - Listen
   - Read summary only
   - Track theme
   - Save for weekly synthesis

## Weekly output should use

Only the strongest items from:

- transcript summaries
- must-listen candidates
- durable ideas saved during the week

Not a recap of every surfaced episode.

## Updated principle

The daily briefing is not:

> “Here’s how the filter worked.”

It is:

> “Here’s what mattered today in the podcast universe, filtered for you.”
```

### assistant

```text
Yes. You’re right.

If the product promise is “scan the podcast world in general,” then Naval should have been found even if he wasn’t already on our seed list.

What actually happened is the current prototype is **not yet a true global podcast-world scan**. It is mostly:

1. **Known RSS sources** from `feeds.yaml`
2. **Resolved feeds** from that source list
3. **Guest watchlist matches inside those feeds**
4. A placeholder Ring 3 concept for broader discovery, but not implemented yet

So the system was behaving like:

> “Scan my configured podcast universe.”

Not yet:

> “Search the wider podcast universe for people and conversations DJ would care about.”

That’s the gap.

## Why Naval slipped through

Naval was absent from both primary discovery surfaces:

- Not in `feeds.yaml`
- Not in `guest_watchlist.yaml`

And Ring 3 global discovery currently exists only as config/query intent:

``​`yaml
ring3_discovery_queries:
  - '"{guest}" podcast'
  - '"{guest}" "Apple Podcasts"'
  - '"{guest}" "Spotify" podcast'
  - '"{guest}" "YouTube" interview'
``​`

…but we haven’t yet built the job that runs those searches daily.

So if Naval wasn’t already in our known feed universe, the collector had no mechanism to discover his own new episodes.

## Correct architecture

We need three daily passes:

### Pass 1 — Known source scan

Scan the configured RSS feeds.

This catches:
- Stratechery
- Dwarkesh
- Odd Lots
- Invest Like the Best
- Acquired
- All-In
- etc.

### Pass 2 — Guest watchlist discovery

For high-value people, search beyond known feeds.

Examples:

- Naval Ravikant
- Benedict Evans
- Ben Thompson
- Brian Chesky
- Jensen Huang
- Stanley Druckenmiller
- Paul Tudor Jones
- Marc Andreessen
- Patrick Collison
- Sam Altman
- Dario Amodei
- etc.

This should search podcast indexes, Apple/Spotify/web/YouTube, and show pages for recent appearances.

### Pass 3 — Theme/event discovery

Search for topics that matter even if the guest/show is unknown.

Examples:

- “AI capex”
- “token budgeting”
- “private evals”
- “agent economy”
- “AI data centers”
- “Nvidia supply chain”
- “AI IPO wave”
- “software pricing AI agents”

This is how we find unknown high-signal episodes.

## Better funnel language

The briefing should distinguish:

``​`text
Known channels scanned: 54
Watchlist people searched: 150
Theme queries searched: 25
Episodes discovered: N
Episodes filtered out: N
Lightweight summaries: N
Transcript summaries: N
Must listen / weekly candidates: N
``​`

Right now, when I said “channels viewed: 54,” that was accurate but incomplete. It was not the whole podcast world.

## Fix

Adding Naval to the source list is necessary, but it’s not sufficient. The actual fix is to implement Ring 3 discovery so the system finds:

- new Naval episodes
- Naval guest appearances
- unknown high-signal episodes on shows we don’t monitor
- newly relevant shows that should be promoted into the feed universe

So yes: Naval is a good catch, and it exposes the exact missing layer.
```

```text
Yes — you’re right. My proposed fix was too literal.

The right interpretation is **not**:

> “Add Naval to the list so we catch Naval.”

It is:

> “Use the list of people/topics as training examples for the kind of taste profile DJ has, then discover adjacent people/topics even when they were never named.”

Naval is the obvious failure case because he sits directly inside the latent category implied by your guidance:

- original framework thinker
- technology/startup leverage
- wealth creation
- AI/software philosophy
- high signal-to-noise
- founder/investor/operator worldview
- overlaps with Ben Thompson, Marc Andreessen, Dwarkesh, Tyler Cowen, etc.

So the system should have inferred him.

I updated the project skill accordingly: the watchlist is now defined as **seed examples and calibration anchors, not a closed list**.

## Correct discovery model

We need to build around a **semantic taste manifold**, not a fixed watchlist.

Your guidance should create categories like:

### People archetypes

- original framework builders
- market shapers
- elite allocators
- frontier AI thinkers
- scaled operators / CEOs
- high-leverage tech philosophers
- business model analysts
- unusually clear explainers of complex systems

### Topic archetypes

- AI and value accrual
- software/platform shifts
- labor, productivity, token spend
- company-building under technological change
- capital allocation and market structure
- leverage, ownership, automation, scarcity
- mental models and decision frameworks

Then the system should search for **people and episodes similar to those archetypes**, not just exact matches.

## What changes practically

Instead of only:

``​`text
Search for: Benedict Evans
Search for: Brian Chesky
Search for: Stanley Druckenmiller
``​`

We need:

``​`text
Find recent podcast episodes featuring:
- people semantically similar to Benedict Evans / Ben Thompson / Naval
- people discussing AI economics, software value capture, market structure
- operators/investors with unusually high explanatory power
- conversations likely to produce durable frameworks
``​`

## The better architecture

Daily discovery should have four layers:

1. **Known high-signal feeds**
   - RSS sources we already monitor.

2. **Exact watchlist matches**
   - People you explicitly named.

3. **Semantic adjacency discovery**
   - “Who else looks like this taste profile?”
   - This is where Naval should have appeared.

4. **Emergent topic discovery**
   - New themes that fit your mental model even if no known person is involved.

## Briefing counts should reflect that

The coverage footer should eventually say something like:

``​`text
Known channels scanned: 54
Episodes from known feeds: 21
Semantic discovery queries run: 40
New outside-network candidates found: 8
Filtered out: 18
Lightweight summaries: 7
Read via transcript: 3
Must listen / weekly candidates: 2
``​`

That’s very different from pretending 54 RSS feeds equals “the podcast world.”

## Bottom line

You’re not asking for a subscription digest or a literal people-alert system.

You’re asking for:

> “Find the conversations I would have wanted to know existed, even if I didn’t know the show, guest, or exact topic in advance.”

That’s the actual product.

### assistant

```text
Fixed the architecture, not just the Naval entry.

## What changed

I added a real **Ring 3 semantic discovery pass**:

`/opt/data/scripts/podcast_semantic_discovery.py`

This now does what you described:

1. Takes your taste profile as a **latent preference model**, not a fixed list.
2. Uses Qwen to generate adjacent:
   - people
   - themes
   - discovery queries
3. Searches podcast indexes beyond the known RSS list.
4. Parses discovered feeds.
5. Scores recent episodes against your actual taste.
6. Inserts strong candidates into the episode database as:

`ring3_semantic_discovery`

So the pipeline is no longer just:

> known feeds + exact watchlist

It now has:

> known feeds + exact watchlist + semantic adjacent discovery

## Smoke test result

I ran semantic discovery.

### 7-day broader test

- Semantic queries run: **35**
- Feeds found: **124**
- Candidate episodes scored: **80**
- Strong candidates inserted: **50**

It found Naval’s episode:

``​`text
Naval — Full Episode: The AI Industrial Revolution
Score: 88
Tier: digest_only / transcript candidate
Link: http://nav.al/industrial
``​`

### 24-hour production-style test

- Semantic queries run: **35**
- Feeds found: **129**
- Candidate episodes scored: **20**
- Strong candidates inserted: **11**

This is now part of the daily workflow.

## Important: I also tightened noise filtering

The first semantic pass found the right kind of stuff, but it also let in some junk from broad search. I cleaned and tightened:

- removed low-quality/non-English aggregator feeds
- filtered crypto/Bitcoin/Web3 noise
- filtered generic Hinton/superintelligence alarmism unless there is a business/investor/operator frame
- raised insertion threshold so weak semantic hits don’t pollute the briefing

I also removed noisy rows already inserted from that first broad pass.

## New daily workflow

Going forward the daily run should be:

``​`bash
python3 /opt/data/scripts/podcast_resolve_collect_rank.py collect --days 1
python3 /opt/data/scripts/podcast_semantic_discovery.py --days 1
python3 /opt/data/scripts/podcast_qwen_episode_score.py --since-hours 24 --tag daily-24h
``​`

Then the briefing uses both:

- known feed episodes
- semantic discovery episodes

## Updated briefing counts should include

``​`text
Known channels scanned: N
Known-feed episodes released: N
Semantic discovery queries run: N
Semantic feeds found: N
Semantic candidates scored: N
Episodes filtered out: N
Lightweight summaries: N
Read via transcript/rich page: N
Must listen / weekly candidates: N
``​`

## Files created/updated

Created:

`/opt/data/scripts/podcast_semantic_discovery.py`

Created discovery DB:

`/opt/data/podcast_digest/semantic_discovery.sqlite`

Latest semantic run output:

`/opt/data/podcast_digest/outputs/2026-06-05_0949-semantic-discovery.json`

Updated workflow docs in the `podcast-intelligence-digest` skill so future runs require semantic discovery.

## Bottom line

You were right: the system was pretending a configured RSS universe was “the podcast world.”

Now it has an actual semantic discovery layer that uses your examples as **training signal**, not as a closed checklist.

Each section shows the `gh` way first, then the `git` + `curl` fallback for machines without `gh`.\n\n## Prerequisites\n\n- Authenticated with GitHub (see `github-auth` skill)\n- Inside a git repository with a GitHub remote\n\n### Quick Auth Detection\n\n``​`bash\n# Determine which method to use throughout this workflow\nif command -v gh &>/dev/null && gh auth status &>/dev/null; then\n  AUTH=\"gh\"\nelse\n  AUTH=\"git\"\n  # Ensure we have a token for API calls\n  if [ -z \"$GITHUB_TOKEN\" ]; then\n    if [ -f ~/.hermes/.env ] && grep -q \"^GITHUB_TOKEN=REDACTED ~/.hermes/.env; then\n      GITHUB_TOKEN=REDACTED \"^GITHUB_TOKEN=REDACTED ~/.hermes/.env | head -1 | cut -d= -f2 | tr -d '\\n\\r')\n    elif grep -q \"github.com\" ~/.git-credentials 2>/dev/null; then\n      GITHUB_TOKEN=REDACTED \"github.com\" ~/.git-credentials 2>/dev/null | head -1 | sed 's|https://[^:]*:\\([^@]*\\)@.*|\\1|')\n    fi\n  fi\nfi\necho \"Using: $AUTH\"\n``​`\n\n### Extracting Owner/Repo from the Git Remote\n\nMany `curl` commands need `owner/repo`. Extract it from the git remote:\n\n``​`bash\n# Works for both HTTPS and SSH remote URLs\nREMOTE_URL=$(git remote get-url origin)\nOWNER_REPO=$(echo \"$REMOTE_URL\" | sed -E 's|.*github\\.com[:/]||; s|\\.git$||')\nOWNER=$(echo \"$OWNER_REPO\" | cut -d/ -f1)\nREPO=$(echo \"$OWNER_REPO\" | cut -d/ -f2)\necho \"Owner: $OWNER, Repo: $REPO\"\n``​`\n\n---\n\n## 1. Branch Creation\n\nThis part is pure `git` — identical either way:\n\n``​`bash\n# Make sure you're up to date\ngit fetch origin\ngit checkout main && git pull origin main\n\n# Create and switch to a new branch\ngit checkout -b feat/add-user-authentication\n``​`\n\nBranch naming conventions:\n- `feat/description` — new features\n- `fix/description` — bug fixes\n- `refactor/description` — code restructuring\n- `docs/description` — documentation\n- `ci/description` — CI/CD changes\n\n## 2. Making Commits\n\nUse the agent's file tools (`write_file`, `patch`) to make changes, then commit:\n\n``​`bash\n# Stage specific files\ngit add src/auth.py src/models/user.py tests/test_auth.py\n\n# Commit with a conventional commit message\ngit commit -m \"feat: add JWT-based user authentication\n\n- Add login/register endpoints\n- Add User model with password hashing\n- Add auth middleware for protected routes\n- Add unit tests for auth flow\"\n``​`\n\nCommit message format (Conventional Commits):\n``​`\ntype(scope): short description\n\nLonger explanation if needed. Wrap at 72 characters.\n``​`\n\nTypes: `feat`, `fix`, `refactor`, `docs`, `test`, `ci`, `chore`, `perf`\n\n## 3. Pushing and Creating a PR\n\n### Push the Branch (same either way)\n\n``​`bash\ngit push -u origin HEAD\n``​`\n\n### Create the PR\n\n**With gh:**\n\n``​`bash\ngh pr create \\\n  --title \"feat: add JWT-based user authentication\" \\\n  --body \"## Summary\n- Adds login and register API endpoints\n- JWT token generation and validation\n\n## Test Plan\n- [ ] Unit tests pass\n\nCloses #42\"\n``​`\n\nOptions: `--draft`, `--reviewer user1,user2`, `--label \"enhancement\"`, `--base develop`\n\n**With git + curl:**\n\n``​`bash\nBRANCH=$(git branch --show-current)\n\ncurl -s -X POST \\\n  -H \"Authorization: token $GITHUB_TOKEN\" \\\n  -H \"Accept: application/vnd.github.v3+json\" \\\n  https://api.github.com/repos/$OWNER/$REPO/pulls \\\n  -d \"{\n    \\\"title\\\": \\\"feat: add JWT-based user authentication\\\",\n    \\\"body\\\": \\\"## Summary\\nAdds login and register API endpoints.\\n\\nCloses #42\\\",\n    \\\"head\\\": \\\"$BRANCH\\\",\n    \\\"base\\\": \\\"main\\\"\n  }\"\n``​`\n\nThe response JSON includes the PR `number` — save it for later commands.\n\nTo create as a draft, add `\"draft\": true` to the JSON body.\n\n## 4. Monitoring CI Status\n\n### Check CI Status\n\n**With gh:**\n\n``​`bash\n# One-shot check\ngh pr checks\n\n# Watch until all checks finish (polls every 10s)\ngh pr checks --watch\n``​`\n\n**With git + curl:**\n\n``​`bash\n# Get the latest commit SHA on the current branch\nSHA=$(git rev-parse HEAD)\n\n# Query the combined status\ncurl -s \\\n  -H \"Authorization: token $GITHUB_TOKEN\" \\\n  https://api.github.com/repos/$OWNER/$REPO/commits/$SHA/status \\\n  | python3 -c \"\nimport sys, json\ndata = json.load(sys.stdin)\nprint(f\\\"Overall: {data['state']}\\\")\nfor s in data.get('statuses', []):\n    print(f\\\"  {s['context']}: {s['state']} - {s.get('description', '')}\\\")\"\n\n# Also check GitHub Actions check runs (separate endpoint)\ncurl -s \\\n  -H \"Authorization: token $GITHUB_TOKEN\" \\\n  https://api.github.com/repos/$OWNER/$REPO/commits/$SHA/check-runs \\\n  | python3 -c \"\nimport sys, json\ndata = json.load(sys.stdin)\nfor cr in data.get('check_runs', []):\n    print(f\\\"  {cr['name']}: {cr['status']} / {cr['conclusion'] or 'pending'}\\\")\"\n``​`\n\n### Poll Until Complete (git + curl)\n\n``​`bash\n# Simple polling loop — check every 30 seconds, up to 10 minutes\nSHA=$(git rev-parse HEAD)\nfor i in $(seq 1 20); do\n  STATUS=$(curl -s \\\n    -H \"Authorization: token $GITHUB_TOKEN\" \\\n    https://api.github.com/repos/$OWNER/$REPO/commits/$SHA/status \\\n    | python3 -c \"import sys,json; print(json.load(sys.stdin)['state'])\")\n  echo \"Check $i: $STATUS\"\n  if [ \"$STATUS\" = \"success\" ] || [ \"$STATUS\" = \"failure\" ] || [ \"$STATUS\" = \"error\" ]; then\n
